Ramsey’s Witnesses
// Restore the saved color scheme preference, or
  // enact the browser preference if "automatic", 
  // without expecting DOM load to have completed.
  //
  // Also restore any saved readingmode and ToC display preferences.
  function initializeReadingPreferences() {
    let saved_theme = localStorage.getItem("ar5iv_theme") || "automatic";
    if (saved_theme === "automatic") {
      if (window.matchMedia("(prefers-color-scheme: dark)").matches) {
        saved_theme = "dark";
      }
    }
    if (saved_theme == "dark") {
      document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-theme", "dark");
    } else {
      document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-theme", "light");
    }

    const tocDisplay = localStorage.getItem('arxiv_html_paper_toc_display');
    if (tocDisplay) {
      document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-toc-display", tocDisplay);
    }
    const readingMode = localStorage.getItem('arxiv_html_paper_reading_mode');
    if (readingMode) {
      document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-reading-mode", readingMode);
    }
  }
  // Run as soon as JS starts, to minimize repainting
  initializeReadingPreferences();
/* Reading mode hides the chrome and the empty .arxiv-html-header marker: the pinned JS reads
     the marker's display to know which way to toggle. The marker draws no box. */
  .arxiv-html-header { display: contents; }
  html[data-reading-mode="enabled"] :is(.arxiv-html-site-chrome, .arxiv-html-header, .ds-site-footer) { display: none; }
  /* The paper controls' icons are blocks, so they sit centred (the pinned JS shows the theme
     icon by making its span a block). */
  .arxiv-html-site-chrome .ds-site-header-tools svg { display: block; width: 18px; height: 18px; flex-shrink: 0; fill: currentColor; }
  /* ar5iv dims every image in dark mode and puts it on a white wash, for paper figures;
     arXiv's own render as they are (the logo on the bar, the funder logos on their white
     chips). Important: ar5iv's dark rules are unlayered, as specific as
     :root:not([data-theme]) img:not(.ltx_no_dark_filter). */
  :is(.ds-site-header, .ds-site-footer) img { filter: none !important; }
  .ds-site-header img { background-color: transparent !important; }

  /* Wide (the theme's 1280px): the theme lays the body out as a grid whose full-width top rows
     were the band's and its sticky reader bar's. The band takes the first and scrolls away; the
     header, the paper's bar (no site navigation, the paper's controls), takes the second and
     stays in view, the one sticky bar, with the anchor clearance that requires (DESIGN-POLICIES).
     The controls show their names. */
  @media (min-width: 1280px) {
    html { scroll-padding-top: 80px; }
    .arxiv-html-site-chrome { display: contents; }
    .arxiv-html-site-chrome > #ds-announcement { grid-area: announce; }
    .arxiv-html-site-chrome > .ds-site-header { grid-area: header; position: sticky; top: 0; z-index: 3; }
    .arxiv-html-site-chrome .tool-icon { display: none; }
  }
  /* Narrow: the header scrolls away, and the controls show icons (names kept for screen
     readers; Why HTML? goes). The theme's contents panel is fixed below where its old sticky
     bar was, and hidden only once the reader has closed it, so until then it is an invisible box
     over the header and the paper: it shows only while open, under the chrome, in its place in
     the page. The paper's footer (build logs, reporting instructions) keeps the text's side
     inset; the theme gives it none, so it met the screen edges. */
  @media (max-width: 1279px) {
    .arxiv-html-site-chrome .tool-wide { display: none; }
    .arxiv-html-site-chrome .tool-label { position: absolute; width: 1px; height: 1px; overflow: hidden; clip: rect(0 0 0 0); white-space: nowrap; }
    html:not([data-toc-display="block"]) .ltx_page_navbar { display: none; }
    .ltx_page_navbar { position: absolute; top: auto; }
    .arxiv-html-footer { padding-inline: 1rem; }
  }

Ramsey’s Witnesses

Abstract
We introduce the notion of
Ramsey partition regularity
, a generalisation of partition regularity involving infinitary configurations.
We provide characterisations of this notion in terms of certain ultrafilters
related to tensor products and dubbed
Ramsey’s witnesses
; and we also consider their nonstandard counterparts
as pairs of hypernatural numbers, called
Ramsey pairs
.
These characterisations are then used to determine whether various configurations involving polynomials and exponentials are Ramsey partition regular over the natural numbers. In particular, this provides negative answers to several questions recently posed by Kra, Moreira, Richter and Robertson.

Introduction
Since the 1970s, applications of ultrafilters to combinatorics have been widely studied,
producing a wealth of results, especially in the area of Ramsey theory; see the comprehensive monograph
[
HS11
]
.
In recent years, the close connection between the space of ultrafilters
β
​
ℕ
\beta\mathbb{N}
and nonstandard extensions
∗
ℕ
{}^{*}\mathbb{N}
of the natural numbers has been thoroughly investigated,
providing an alternative approach that has revealed useful for formalising Ramsey properties
in a simple and manageable way, and that has also made it possible to prove some new
results in the area of
arithmetic Ramsey theory
.
The latter is a branch of combinatorics concerned with the “abundance” of certain arithmetic patterns in the natural numbers. A typical problem in this area is to determine whether a given configuration is ubiquitous enough that one is forced to find instances of it in some piece of every finite partition of
ℕ
\mathbb{N}
.
In this area, it is customary to call partitions
colourings
. For instance, a classical theorem by I. Schur
[
Sch16
]
states that, whenever the natural numbers are coloured with finitely many colours, say
ℕ
=
C
1
∪
…
∪
C
r
\mathbb{N}={C}_{1}\cup\ldots\cup{C}_{r}
, then one of the
C
i
C_{i}
contains a triple of distinct elements
a
,
b
,
c
a,b,c
such that
a
+
b
=
c
a+b=c
. Such a triple is called a
monochromatic
solution of the equation
x
+
y
=
z
x+y=z
, and the existence of monochromatic solutions in all colourings is the statement that the aforementioned equation is
partition regular
.
It is natural to ask which other equations have the same property. Albeit the linear case is well-understood by a classical result of R. Rado
[
Rad33
]
, the polynomial one is still very far from being settled; for instance, whether the Pythagorean equation
x
2
+
y
2
=
z
2
x^{2}+y^{2}=z^{2}
is partition regular is still unknown, and one of the long-standing open problems in the area.
The notion of partition regularity is not limited to solutions to Diophantine equations. In general, a family
ℱ
\mathcal{F}
of subsets of a set
X
X
is
partition regular
(PR) over
X
X
if it is closed under taking supersets and, for all finite colourings
X
=
C
1
∪
…
∪
C
r
X=C_{1}\cup\ldots\cup C_{r}
there exists
i
≤
r
i\leq r
such that
C
i
∈
ℱ
C_{i}\in\mathcal{F}
; and it is
strongly partition regular
(SPR) over
X
X
if, furthermore, for all
Y
∈
ℱ
Y\in\mathcal{F}
the family
ℱ
∩
𝒫
⁡
(
Y
)
\mathcal{F}\cap\mathcal{P}(Y)
is PR over
Y
Y
, i.e. if for all finite colourings
Y
=
C
1
∪
…
∪
C
r

there exists
i
≤
r
i\leq r
such that
C
i
∈
ℱ
C_{i}\in\mathcal{F}
.
If
P

P

is PR if the family
ℱ
P
≔
{

⊆

∣

is PR. So, for example, Schur’s Theorem says that the property of containing some
x

y

with
x
+
y
=
z
x+y=z
is partition regular. Another celebrated example is van der Waerden’s Theorem
[

]
, establishing strong partition regularity for containing arbitrarily long arithmetic progressions.
Problems in arithmetic Ramsey theory have been tackled by employing a variety of methods; as mentioned above, one that attracted significant attention in recent years is to exploit the strict connection between spaces of ultrafilters and their algebra and
nonstandard extensions
of
ℕ
\mathbb{N}
. We now briefly recall what this amounts to; see e.g.
[

,

]
for a more detailed treatment.
The link between partition regularity of a family
ℱ
\mathcal{F}
of subsets of
X

and ultrafilters on
X

stems from the well-known facts that
1.
ℱ
\mathcal{F}
is PR over
X

if and only if there exists an ultrafilter

over

such that

⊆
ℱ

; such a

is called a

of the partition regularity of
ℱ

; and

ℱ

containing

.
As for nonstandard extensions of
ℕ
\mathbb{N}
, recall that these are

of the natural numbers, that is, larger structures satisfying the

for first-order formulas.
1
1
1
Here

is equipped with its full first-order structure, that is, with relation symbols for every subset of each of its Cartesian powers.
For example, this means that, for every subset

ℕ

∗

Over the years Schur’s result has been strengthened in various directions.
Most notably, in

Theorem

Theorem

Typically these statements are closely related, in one way or another, to Ramsey’s Theorem

Because of the infinitary nature of these statements,
Fact

Theorems

demonstrate that being Ramsey PR significantly strengthens being merely PR. One may wonder what happens to other properties classically known to be PR. In this direction, another application of our results is a proof that van der Waerden’s Theorem does not admit a “Ramsey version”, not even in the case of

The paper is structured as follows. In
Section
2
we will prove several properties of Ramsey pairs and characterise them in terms of tensor pairs. In
Section

, we introduce several versions of Ramsey partition regularity and characterise them in ultrafilter and nonstandard terms. In

Section

B
and
C

Section
6
we collect some open problems that we believe deserve further study.

In this paper, we are not going to study general properties of block partition regularity; in fact, as said in the introduction, we are mainly interested to use Ramsey pairs to study “infinitary statements” in combinatorics close in spirit to
Theorem
(Ramsey’s Theorem for pairs)
.
. “Infinitary statements” is a vague concept, that could be formalised in several different ways; in this paper, we define it as the following strengthening of block partition regularity:

6
Open problems
The results obtained in this paper open some lines of enquiry that we believe worth investigating further.
Puritz’ Theorem (
Fact
2.5
) gives a simple characterisation of tensor pairs in terms of standard functions. As for Ramsey pairs, in
Proposition
2.13
, we have shown that they have several properties that can be expressed in terms of standard functions. Most of our applications actually used some of these properties, so finding a Puritz-like characterisation of Ramsey pairs might be helpful in studying other similar combinatorial problems.
Problem 6.1
.
Characterise Ramsey pairs in the style of Puritz’ Theorem.
Problem
6.1
and nonstandard characterisations of related Ramsey properties are the topic of a paper in preparation. Another interesting question regards polynomial equations.
Problem 6.2
.
Is it possible to extend
Corollary
5.10
from monomials to arbitrary polynomials in
x
x
? To arbitrary multivariate polynomials?
Our proof of
Corollary
5.10
uses the fact that it is simple to write the valuation of a monomial in
α
\alpha
in terms of the valuation of
α
\alpha
; the same does not hold for a generic polynomial in
α
\alpha
. An extension of
Corollary
5.10
to arbitrary polynomials in
x
x
would give an analogous extension of its corollaries, and in particular of
Proposition
5.15
.
As observed in
Remark
5.17
, some variants of the
Theorem
(Pairwise Sum-Product Theorem)
.
remain open. The most interesting ones seem to be the following. We suspect that solving these should provide enough information to complete the classification.
Problem 6.3
.
Which of these formulas are Ramsey PR in
(
x
,
y
)
|
z
,
t
(x,y)\mid z,t
?
1.
(
−
x
+
y
=
z
)
∧
(
x
​
y
=
t
)
(-x+y=z)\land(xy=t)
.
2.
(
−
x
+
y
=
z
)
∧
(
y
=
t
​
x
)
(-x+y=z)\land(y=tx)
.
3.
(
x
+
y
=
z
)
∧
(
y
=
t
​
x
)
(x+y=z)\land(y=tx)
.
Our final question essentially asks to deal with the “never considered” cases of
Tables
1
,
2
and
3
from
Section
4
.
Problem 6.4
.
In which partitions of the variables is the formula
(
x
≠
y
)
∧
(
x
+
y
=
z
)
∧
(
x
⋅
y
=
t
)
\left(x\neq y\right)\wedge\left(x+y=z\right)\wedge\left(x\cdot y=t\right)
BPR? In which ones is it Ramsey PR?
